**Iran**,[^cite_note-12] officially the **Islamic Republic of Iran**,[^cite_note-13] and historically known as **Persia**,[^cite_note-15] is a country in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia). It borders Iraq to the west; Turkey, Azerbaijan, and Armenia to the northwest; the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md) to the north, Turkmenistan to the northeast; Afghanistan to the east; Pakistan to the southeast; and the [Gulf of Oman](12896.md) and the [Persian Gulf](24761.md) to the south. With a population of over 92 million, Iran ranks 17th globally in both [geographic size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) and [population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population). It is divided into [five regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Iran) with [31 provinces](448160.md). [Tehran](57654.md) is the nation's [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitals_of_Iran) and [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Iran_by_province) and serves as its primary economic center.&#x20;

Home to one of the world's oldest continuous major civilizations, the territory of present-day Iran was first unified under the [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes) in the 7th century BC and reached its territorial height in the 6th century BC, when [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great) founded the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire). [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) conquered the empire in the 4th century BC.[^cite_note-16] An Iranian rebellion in the 3rd century BC established the [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire), which later liberated the country. In the 3rd century AD, the Parthians were succeeded by the [Sasanian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire), which oversaw a golden age in the [history of Iranian civilization](64213.md). Ancient Iran saw some of the earliest developments of writing, agriculture, urbanization, religion, and administration. Once a center for [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism), Iran underwent [Islamization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_Iran) following the [7th century Muslim conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia). Innovations in [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_philosophy), mathematics, [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Iranian_medicine), astronomy and [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_art) were renewed during the [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age) and [Iranian Intermezzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Intermezzo), a period during which [Iranian Muslim dynasties](13877.md#Iranian_Intermezzo_\(821%E2%80%931090\)) ended Arab rule and revived the [Persian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language). This era was followed by [Seljuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire) and [Khwarazmian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_Empire) rule, [Mongol conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_conquest_of_Persia_and_Mesopotamia) and the [Timurid Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Renaissance) from the 11th to 14th centuries.&#x20;

In the 16th century, the native [Safavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran) re-established a unified Iranian state with [Twelver Shia Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver_Shi%27ism) as the [official religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion), laying the framework for the modern state of Iran. During the [Afsharid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_Iran) in the 18th century, Iran was a leading world power, but it lost this status after the [Qajars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_Iran) took power in the 1790s. The early 20th century saw the [Persian Constitutional Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Constitutional_Revolution) and the establishment of the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md) by [Reza Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah), who ousted the last Qajar Shah in 1925. Following the [Anglo-Soviet invasion of Iran in 1941](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_invasion_of_Iran), his son [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md) rose to power. Attempts by [Mohammad Mosaddegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mosaddegh) to [nationalize the oil industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalization_of_the_Iranian_oil_industry) led to the [Anglo-American coup in 1953](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat).&#x20;

The [Iranian Revolution](347268.md) in 1979 overthrew the monarchy, and the Islamic Republic of Iran was established by [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md), the country's first [supreme leader](36814344.md). In 1980, [Iraq invaded Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_invasion_of_Iran), sparking the eight-year-long [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War). Iran has since been involved in [proxy wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war) with [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Israel_proxy_conflict) and [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_war); in 2025, Israeli strikes on Iran escalated tensions into the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War). Following the war and amid a [growing economic crisis](78620967.md), the [largest protests since 1979](81976180.md) erupted in 2025. The United States and Israel launched a [war with Iran](82533582.md) in 2026, after they [assassinated](82537558.md) supreme leader [Ali Khamenei](385653.md). His son, [Mojtaba Khamenei](23324096.md), was [elected](59042456.md) as his successor.&#x20;

Iran's government is a [theocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocracy#Islamic_theocracies) [Islamic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_republic) governed by elected and unelected institutions, with ultimate authority vested in the supreme leader. While [it holds elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Iran), key offices, including the head of state and military, are not subject to public vote. The Iranian government is an [authoritarian regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) which has been widely criticized internationally due to [poor human rights record](3156692.md), including restrictions on [freedom of assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_assembly), [expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_expression_in_Iran), and the [press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Iran), as well as its [treatment of women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_rights_in_Iran), [ethnic minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_minorities_in_Iran), and [political dissidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_prisoners_in_Iran). The [Iranian economy](64704.md) is centrally planned with significant state ownership in key sectors. It is a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power), due to its large reserves of [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel), [natural gas supply](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_in_Iran) and [oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_proven_oil_reserves), its geopolitically significant location, and its role as the world's focal point of [Shia Islam](26961.md).[^cite_note-The_United_States_and_the_Great_Powers-17] Iran is a [threshold state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_latency) with [one of the most scrutinized nuclear programs](721807.md).[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19] It is a founding member of the UN and [a member state of numerous international organizations](64709.md). Iran has 30 [UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Iran) (the [10th-highest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites_by_country)) and ranks 4th in [intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_cultural_heritage) or human treasures.&#x20;

## Etymology

This section is an excerpt from [Name of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Iran).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Name_of_Iran\&action=edit)]



A map of [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) in 1872, with "Iran or Persia", ruled by the [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty), shaded in pink.

Historically, Iran was commonly referred to as "Persia" in the [Western world](21208200.md).[^cite_note-20] Likewise, the modern-day [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym) "Persian" was typically used as a [demonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonym) for all Iranian nationals, regardless of whether or not they were [ethnic Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persians). This terminology prevailed until 1935, when, during an international gathering for [Nowruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz), the [Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah) of Iran, [Reza Shah Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah) officially requested that foreign delegates begin using the endonym ["Iran"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_\(word\)) in formal correspondence. Subsequently, "Iran" and "Iranian" were standardised as the terms referring to the country and its citizens, respectively.[^cite_note-Name_of_Iran_yarshater1-21]&#x20;

In 1959, the last Iranian Shah, [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md), announced that it was appropriate to use both "Persia" and "Iran" in formal correspondence.[^cite_note-Name_of_Iran_yarshater1-21]&#x20;

A variety of scholars from the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), such as the [Khwarazmian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazm) polymath [Al-Biruni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Biruni), also used terms like "[Xuniras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xuniras)" ([Avestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avestan_language): *Xvaniraθa-*, transl. "self-made, not resting on anything else") to refer to Iran: "which is the center of the world, \[...] and it is the one wherein we are, and the kings called it the [Iranian realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_realm)."[^cite_note-Name_of_Iran_\:0-22]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Iran](64213.md)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Iranian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Iranian_history).

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistory of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Iran)

The earliest known presence of hominins in Iran dates to around 800,000 years ago, in the [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic).[^cite_note-bishi-23] Many [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic) sites have been discovered, mainly in the [Zagros Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagros_Mountains) in western Iran and some sites associated with [Neanderthals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal).[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] The [Zarzian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarzian_culture) is documented in Iran during the [Epipaleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epipaleolithic) (25,000–11,500 years ago).[^cite_note-26] Agriculture first appeared in Iran some 12,000 years ago alongside better-documented settlements in the [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent). The [Chogha Golan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chogha_Golan) site featured early domestication of [emmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmer) wheat.[^cite_note-27] The contemporary site of [Ganj Dareh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganj_Dareh) also features the earliest known domestication of [goats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goat) around 10,000 years ago.[^cite_note-28]&#x20;

The ancient city of [Susa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susa), which would become the capital of [Elam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elam) and later a capital city of the [Achaemenid empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_empire), was first settled in 4400–4200 BC, adjacent to the modern site of [Shush, Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shush,_Iran).[^cite_note-Elam-29]<sup>: 46–47 </sup>[^cite_note-30] The [Kura–Araxes culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kura%E2%80%93Araxes_culture) (c. 3400 – c. 2000 BC) existed in northwestern Iran and the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus).[^cite_note-books.google.nl2-31]&#x20;

### Antiquity

Further information: [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes) and [Indo-Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Iranians)



[Chogha Zanbil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chogha_Zanbil) is one of the few extant [ziggurats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziggurat) outside of [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) and considered the best-preserved example in the world.

Iran is home to one of the world's oldest continuous major civilizations, with historical and urban settlements dating back to 4000 BC, including the [Jiroft culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiroft_culture) in southeastern Iran.[^cite_note-32] Inscriptions in the [Proto-Elamite script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Elamite_script), which predates [cuneiform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuneiform), have been found from the early third millennium BC.[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34] The western part of the Iranian plateau participated in the traditional [ancient Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Near_East) with [Elam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elam) (3200–539 BC), and later with other peoples such as the [Kassites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kassites), [Mannaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mannaea), and [Gutians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gutians). The earliest Iranian peoples began to arrive from Central Asia in the 2nd millennium BC.[^cite_note-35]&#x20;

The [Median dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_dynasty#Genealogy) ruled the earliest Iranian state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDandamayev20211630-36][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKatouzian2013xii-37][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaube2008161-38] In 612 BC, [Cyaxares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyaxares) and the [Babylonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia) king [Nabopolassar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabopolassar) invaded [Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria) and destroyed [Nineveh](21699.md), the Assyrian capital, which led to the fall of the [Neo-Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian_Empire).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESicker200068–69-39] The Medes later conquered and dissolved [Urartu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urartu) as well.[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShenkar20145-42]&#x20;

#### Achaemenid Empire

Main article: [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire)



The [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire), or the First Persian Empire, at its greatest extent

The [Achaemenids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_dynasty) united all [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persians) tribes under [Cambyses I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambyses_I). Under his son, [Cyrus the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great), the Achaemenids defeated the Medes and established the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiddleton2015148-43] the largest-ever Iranian state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShenkar20145-42] Cyrus conquered the [Lydian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydia) and [Neo-Babylonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Babylonian_Empire) empires, creating an empire far larger than Assyria. His son, [Cambyses II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambyses_II) (r. 530–522 BC), conquered the last major power of the region, [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt), causing the collapse of its [twenty-sixth dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-sixth_Dynasty_of_Egypt).&#x20;

After the death of Cambyses II, [Darius the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_the_Great) (r. 522–486 BC) ascended the throne by overthrowing the Achaemenid monarch [Bardiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bardiya). Darius' first capital was at Susa, and he started the building program at [Persepolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persepolis). He improved the extensive road system, and during his reign the first recorded mentions are made of the [Royal Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Road), a highway from Susa to [Sardis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardis).[^cite_note-44]&#x20;

In 499 BC, [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Athens) supported a revolt in [Miletus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miletus), resulting in the sacking of Sardis. This led to the [Greco-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Persian_Wars), which lasted the first half of the 5th century BC. In the [First Persian invasion of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Persian_invasion_of_Greece), Persian general [Mardonius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardonius_\(general\)) re-subjugated Thrace and made Macedon a full part of Persia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoismanWorthington2011345-45] Darius' successor [Xerxes I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerxes_I) (r. 486–465) launched the [Second Persian invasion of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Persian_invasion_of_Greece). At a crucial moment in the war, about half of mainland Greece was overrun by the Persians, including territories to the north of the Isthmus of [Corinth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Corinth).[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAeschylus200918-47] This was reversed by a Greek victory following the battles of [Plataea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Plataea) and [Salamis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Salamis), during which Persia lost all of its footholds in Europe, and withdrew from it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoismanWorthington2011135–138,_342–345-48]&#x20;

The empire entered a period of decline. From 334 BC to 331 BC, [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) defeated [Darius III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_III) (r. 336–330 BC) in the battles of [Granicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Granicus), [Issus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Issus) and [Gaugamela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaugamela), swiftly conquering the Achaemanid Empire by 331 BC. Alexander's empire collapsed after his death; his general, [Seleucus I Nicator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucus_I_Nicator), tried to take control of Iran, [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), [Syria](7515849.md), and [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia). His empire was the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire).&#x20;

#### Parthian and Sasanian empires

Main articles: [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire) and [Sasanian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire)

The Arsacids of [Parthia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthia),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVenning2023118-49] initially Seleucid vassals,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrootman2020151-50] originated as leaders of the Iranian[^cite_note-53] [Parni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parni) tribe in the northeastern [steppes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVenning2023162-54] The Parthians gradually [challenged Seleucid rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid%E2%80%93Parthian_Wars) over Iran, eventually securing control through the 142 BC conquest of [Babylonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVenning2023118-49][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrootman2020150-55] Although fighting continued, the death of [Antiochus VII Sidetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochus_VII_Sidetes) in 129 BC marked the collapse of the Seleucid Empire,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrootman2020150-55] which then lingered on as a [rump state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rump_state) in Syria until conquered by the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) in the 60s BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVenning2023118-49]&#x20;



The [Sasanian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) at its greatest extent c. 620, under the reign of [Khosrow II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khosrow_II)

The [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire) endured for five centuries, but civil wars destabilized it. Parthian power evaporated when [Ardashir I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardashir_I) revolted against the Arsacids and killed their last ruler, [Artabanus IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artabanus_IV_of_Parthia), in 224 AD. Ardashir established the [Sasanian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire), which ruled Iran and much of [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahbazi2005-56] At their zenith, the Sasanians controlled all of modern-day Iran and Iraq and parts of the [Arabian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia), as well as the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus), the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant), and parts of [Central](6742.md) and [South Asia](21566765.md).[^cite_note-57]&#x20;

The strong economic conditions left by Parthians allowed the Sasanians to build a powerful and distinctive economic state whose reputation spread well beyond its political frontiers and time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGyselen1997-58] The Sasanian Empire was characterized by a complex and centralized government bureaucracy and the revitalization of [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism) as a legitimizing and unifying ideal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEiland200481-59]&#x20;

### Medieval period

Main articles: [Iranian Intermezzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Intermezzo), [Tahirid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahirid_dynasty), [Saffarid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffarid_dynasty), [Ziyarid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziyarid_dynasty), [Samanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanids), [Sajid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajid_dynasty), [Sallarid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sallarid_dynasty), [Ilyasids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilyasids), [Buyid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buyid_dynasty), [Kakuyids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakuyids), [Seljuk Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire), [Khwarazmian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_Empire), and [Ilkhanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate)

Most of the Sasanian Empire's lifespan was overshadowed by the frequent [Byzantine–Sasanian wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Sasanian_wars), a continuation of the [Roman–Parthian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Parthian_Wars). The last of these wars ultimately contributed to the [empire's fall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Sasanian_Empire), which culminated with the [Islamic conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia). The [Rashidun Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) conquered the Sasanian Empire between 632 and 654.&#x20;

Over time, the majority of Iranians converted to [Islam](6037917.md). Most of the aspects of the previous Persian civilizations were not discarded, but rather absorbed by the new Islamic polity.[^cite_note-lewis-60]&#x20;

#### Early Islamic rule and regional resistance in Iran

After the fall of the Sasanian Empire in 651, the [Arabs](2185.md) of the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) adopted many Persian customs, especially the administrative and the court mannerisms. Arab provincial governors were undoubtedly either Persianized [Arameans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans) or ethnic Persians; certainly, [Middle Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Persian_language) remained the language of official business of the caliphate until the adoption of Arabic toward the end of the seventh century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHawting198663–64-61]&#x20;

However, Iran was still not entirely under Arab control; the [Daylam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daylam) region was under the control of the [Daylamites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daylamites), [Tabaristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabaristan) was under [Dabuyid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dabuyid_dynasty) and [Paduspanid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paduspanids) control, and [Mount Damavand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Damavand) under [Masmughan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masmughans_of_Damavand) control. Arabs had invaded these regions several times but the regions' inaccessible terrain prevented a decisive result. The most prominent ruler of the Dabuyids, [Farrukhan the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farrukhan_the_Great) (r. 712–728), managed to hold his domains during his long struggle against the Arab general [Yazid ibn al-Muhallab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazid_ibn_al-Muhallab), who was defeated by a combined Daylamite–Dabuyid army and forced to retreat from Tabaristan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPourshariati2008312–313-62]&#x20;

#### The Abbasid revolution and Iranian Renaissance



The [Saffarid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffarid_dynasty) in 900 AD



A map of Iranian dynasties in the mid 10th-century

Anti-Umayyad insurrections were supported by non-Arab Islamic converts, who were resentful over being relegated to lower social standing. In 747–750, one of these insurrections grew into the [Abbasid revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_revolution), in which the Umayyads were replaced with the [Abbasids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_dynasty), descendants of Muhammad's uncle, [Abbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_ibn_Abd_al-Muttalib).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsman201462–63-63]&#x20;

The political authority of the [Abbasid caliphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) diminished over the course of the 9th and 10th centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELorentz2007xxviii-64] This led to the establishment of several independent Iranian dynasties,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELorentz2007xxviii-64] the ousting of Arab rulers from their scattered bastions across the country, and an Iranian cultural renaissance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMahendrarajah2019-65] The period between the collapse of Abbasid authority and the conquest of Iran by the [Seljuk Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire) in the 11th century is referred to as the "[Iranian Intermezzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Intermezzo)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMahendrarajah2019-65]&#x20;

The Iranian Intermezzo saw the rise and fall of several major and minor dynasties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMahendrarajah2019-65] Among the most significant of these overlapping dynasties were the [Tahirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahirid_dynasty) in [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Khorasan) (821–873); the [Saffarids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffarid_dynasty) in [Sistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistan) (861–1003); and the [Samanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanid_Empire) (819–1005), originally at [Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukhara). The Samanids eventually ruled an area from central Iran to Pakistan.&#x20;

By the early 10th century, the Abbasids almost lost control to the growing Iranian faction known as the [Buyid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buyid_dynasty) (934–1062).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlair19926-66] Since much of the Abbasid administration had been Persian, the Buyids were quietly able to assume real power in Baghdad. The Buyids were defeated in the mid-11th century by the [Seljuk Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire), who continued to exert influence over the Abbasids. The Seljuks were eventually supplanted by the [Khwarazmian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_Empire) in 1194, a culturally Persianate empire of [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) *[mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk)* origin.[^cite_note-67]&#x20;

#### Islamization and Persianization

The [Islamization of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_Iran) was a long process. As Persian Muslims consolidated their rule, the Muslim population rose from approximately 40% in the mid-9th century to close to 90% by the end of the 11th century. Historian [Seyyed Hossein Nasr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seyyed_Hossein_Nasr) suggests that the rapid increase in conversion was aided by the Persian nationality of the rulers.[^cite_note-68] Although Persians adopted the religion of their conquerors, over the centuries they worked to protect and revive their distinctive language and culture, a process known as [Persianization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persianization). Arabs and Turks participated in this process.[^cite_note-britannica2-69][^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71]&#x20;

#### Mongol invasions

See also: [Mongol invasion of the Khwarazmian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_the_Khwarazmian_Empire) and [Mongol conquest of Persia and Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_conquest_of_Persia_and_Mesopotamia)



Territory of the [Khwarazmian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_Empire) on the eve of the [Mongol conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_campaigns_in_Central_Asia), c. 1215

In the early 13th century, the Mongols reached Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWard200939-72] [Bukhara was conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Bukhara) in 1220[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAigle202426-73] and the [Khwarazmian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_Empire) was destroyed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBosworth2009-74] During 1220–21 Bukhara, [Samarkand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Samarkand_\(1220\)), [Herat](14128.md), [Tus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tus,_Iran) and [Nishapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nishapur) were razed, and the whole populations were slaughtered. Over the following decades, further conquests followed, culminating in the [fall of Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Baghdad) and end of the [Abbasid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate)'s rule in 1258.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELane2012250-75]&#x20;

After the death of [Möngke Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B6ngke_Khan), the [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) was fractured by civil war, both over the succession of the next Great Khan and between nomadic traditionalists and the new settled princes of China and the Middle East. [Kublai Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kublai_Khan) was eventually universally recognized, but the empire was irreversibly fragmented.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELane2012250-75] In much of the southwest of the empire, including Iran, power fell to [Hulegu Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hulegu_Khan),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossabi200232-76] who had been made a deputy there under Möngke Khan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELane2012250-75] Hulegu was accepted as a legitimate ruler in Iran and was legitimized through a *[fatwa](11259.md)* issued by the Shia scholar [Ali ibn Tawus al-Hilli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_ibn_Tawus_al-Hilli).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELane2012253–254,_256-77] Iran experienced a cultural renaissance under [Ilkhanid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate) rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELane2012253–254,_256-77] [Ghazan Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazan_Khan) converted to Islam in the late 13th century, turning the state further away from the other Mongol realms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossabi200232-76]&#x20;

After Ghazan's nephew [Abu Said](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sa%27id_Bahadur_Khan) died in 1335, the Ilkhanate lapsed into civil war and was divided between several petty dynasties – most prominently the [Jalayirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalayirids), [Muzaffarids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muzaffarids_\(Iran\)), [Sarbadars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbadars) and [Kartids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartids). The mid-14th-century [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) killed about 30% of the country's population.[^cite_note-78]&#x20;

#### Timur and the rise of new powers

See also: [Timurid conquests and invasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_conquests_and_invasions)



[Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur)'s empire and his military campaigns

Iran remained divided until the establishment of the Persianate [Timurid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_dynasty) in 1370. Its founder, Timur (r. 1370–1405), hailed from a [Turkified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) tribe of Mongols.[^cite_note-79] After establishing a power base in [Transoxiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transoxiana), Timur invaded Iran in 1381 and eventually conquered most of it. Timur's campaigns were known for their brutality; many people were slaughtered and several cities were destroyed.[^cite_note-lcweb2.loc.gov-80]&#x20;

In 1387, Timur ordered the complete [massacre of Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Isfahan_\(1387\)), killing 70,000 people.[^cite_note-81] The Timurids maintained control of most of Iran until 1452, when they lost the bulk of it to the [Qara Qoyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qara_Qoyunlu), who were conquered by the [Aq Qoyunlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aq_Qoyunlu) in 1468. [Uzun Hasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzun_Hasan) and his successors were the masters of Iran until the rise of the Safavids.[^cite_note-lcweb2.loc.gov-80]&#x20;

### Early modern period

Main articles: [Safavid Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran), [Afsharid Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_Iran), [Zand dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zand_dynasty), [Qajar Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_Iran), [Portuguese–Safavid wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese%E2%80%93Safavid_wars), and [Ottoman–Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Persian_Wars)



[Safavid Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran) (1501–1722) at its greatest extent

The [Safavid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran), founded by [Ismail I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_I) (r. 1505–1524), is often considered the beginning of modern Iranian history.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthee2008-82] While Safavid Iran predated the concept of the nation-state as such, it established the basis for the modern state of Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthee2008-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2011432-83] The [Safavid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_dynasty) dynastic period was one of the most significant in Iranian history, as it reunified Iran as a cohesive entity under native rule and established Shia Islam as the [official religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESavoryKaramustafa1998-84] The Safavids ruled from 1501 to 1722, and experienced a brief restoration from 1729 to 1736.&#x20;

The Safavid state had a complex bureaucratic system of checks and balances, which ensured transparency and prevented fraud. This system was not intended to equalize power between branches of government, but to ensure total power of the shah. Legitimized by his bloodline as a [sayyid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid), or descendant of Muhammad, the shah monitored the actions of government officials through reports provided by the superintendent of each department. [Jean Chardin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Chardin), French merchant and eventual ambassador to Iran, wrote that the Safavid shahs ruled their land with an iron fist and often in a [despotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Despotism) manner.[^cite_note-85]&#x20;

Complex rivalries in the region of [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Khorasan) led to the Afghan [Hotak dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotak_dynasty) invading Iran. In 1722, this conflict led to the collapse of the Safavid Empire after the [siege of Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Isfahan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatthee2008-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAghaie2012306-86] The brief interlude between 1722 and the rise of the [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) in 1789–1796 was marked by widespread political turmoil in Iran and several rival attempts to establish power over the country. The Safavids failed to regain power and the Hotaks failed to establish control. The rival [Afsharid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_Iran) and [Zand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zand_dynasty) dynasties were established by [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah) (r. 1736–1747) and [Karim Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Khan_Zand) (r. 1751–1779), respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAghaie2012306-86]&#x20;

#### Nader Shah and the shifting balance of power

See also: [Campaigns of Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaigns_of_Nader_Shah)



A map of the [Afsharid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_Empire) at its greatest extent, in 1741–1745

Nader Shah has been described as "the last great Asiatic military conqueror", and compared by some historians to Napoleon and Alexander the Great.[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAxworthy2008xvii-88] His numerous campaigns created a great empire that, at its maximum extent, briefly encompassed all or part of modern-day Afghanistan, Armenia, Azerbaijan, Bahrain, Georgia, India, Iran, Iraq, Turkey, Turkmenistan, Oman, Pakistan, Uzbekistan, the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus), and the [Persian Gulf](24761.md). However, his military spending had a ruinous effect on the Iranian economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETucker2006a-89]&#x20;

Nader Shah's death was followed by a [period of anarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_the_Afsharid_Empire) in Iran as rival army commanders fought for power. Nader's own family, the [Afsharids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_dynasty), lost all but a small domain in Khorasan. The Zand family seized control of much of Iran in the 1750s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200513-90] The Zand rulers never proclaimed themselves to be *shah*s, but ruled as [regents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regent) of Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200513-90]*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerry2000-91]* They first staked their claim to power on behalf of the Safavid puppet [Ismail III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_III) (r. 1750–1773) and then on behalf of the Iranian people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200513-90]*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerry2000-91]*&#x20;

#### Rise of the Qajar dynasty and foreign interference



Iran under the [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) in the 19th century.

The Qajar dynasty gradually increased in power as they clashed with the Afsharids and the Zands for control of the land, culminating in [Agha Mohammad Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agha_Mohammad_Shah) proclaiming himself ruler in 1789.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerry1984-92] Agha Mohammad defeated the Zand dynasty in 1794 and was officially crowned in 1796.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker200513-90][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerry1984-92] Shortly thereafter, he captured and deposed the Afsharid Shahrokh Shah, reunifying Iran under a single ruler.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerry1984-92]&#x20;

In the 19th century, Iran lost significant territories in the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) to the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) following the [Russo-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_Wars).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowling2014728–729-93] Meanwhile, [Britain](31717.md) became involved in [southern Iran](36042664.md) to counter Russia's presence in the north, which posed a threat to [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_India).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmanat2002-94] Amid this struggle for power, drought, shifting agricultural priorities, and poor governance culminated in the [Great Persian Famine of 1870–1871](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_famine_of_1870%E2%80%931872). While the death toll is unknown, the famine killed a significant portion of Iran's population; between several hundred thousand and four million Iranians are believed to have died as a result.[^cite_note-95]&#x20;

### 20th century up to the Iranian Revolution

Main article: [Pahlavi Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran)

#### Constitutional Revolution and the rise of the Pahlavi dynasty



[Reza Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah) with then-Crown Prince Mohammad Reza Pahlavi at [Persepolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persepolis), c. 1939

The [Persian Constitutional Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Constitutional_Revolution) between 1905 and 1911 led to the establishment of an Iranian parliament.[^cite_note-96] After the [1921 coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1921_Persian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat), the Qajar dynasty was replaced with the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md).[^cite_note-97] The dynasty was founded by [Reza Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Shah), who established an [authoritarian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) that valued [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_nationalism), [militarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militarism), [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_in_Iran) and [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) combined with strict [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship) and [state propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_propaganda).[^cite_note-98] Reza Shah introduced many socio-economic reforms, reorganizing the army, government administration, and finances.[^cite_note-Columbia_Encyclopedia-99] Reza Shah ruled for almost 16 years until 1941, when he was forced to [abdicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdication) by the [Anglo-Soviet invasion of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_invasion_of_Iran).&#x20;

To his supporters, his reign brought "[law and order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_order_\(politics\)), discipline, central authority, and modern amenities – schools, trains, buses, radios, cinemas, and telephones."[^cite_note-Ervand,_2008_p.91-100] However, his reign has been characterized as a corrupt [police state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_state) which provided only surface level modernization.[^cite_note-Ervand,_2008_p.91-100][^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102]&#x20;

#### World War II and post-occupation instability

Due in part to Nazi Germany's [invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa) of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), the Iranian government expected Germany to win the war and establish a powerful force on the Russian-Iranian border. Iran rejected British and Soviet demands to expel German residents from its borders. In response, the two [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) invaded in August 1941 and easily overwhelmed the weak Iranian army in [Operation Countenance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Countenance).[^cite_note-Richard_Stewart_1988-103]&#x20;

Iran became the major conduit of Allied [Lend-Lease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lend-Lease) aid to the Soviet Union. The purpose was to secure Iranian oil fields and ensure Allied supply lines through the [Persian Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Corridor). Iran remained officially neutral. Reza Shah was deposed during the subsequent occupation and replaced with his young son [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md).[^cite_note-Richard_Stewart_1988-103] At the 1943 [Tehran Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Conference), the Allied "Big Three"—[Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin), [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt), and [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill)—issued the Tehran Declaration to guarantee the post-war independence and boundaries of Iran.[^cite_note-104]&#x20;

#### Mosaddegh and the Shah's rule

See also: [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat)



[The coronation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_Mohammad_Reza_Pahlavi) of [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md), the last [Iranian coronation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_the_Iranian_monarch), held in 1967



[Mohammad Reza Shah](20065598.md) and Shahbanu [Farah Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farah_Pahlavi) of [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran) with US President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) and [King Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Hussein) of Jordan in 1977

In 1951, under Prime Minister [Mohammad Mosaddegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mosaddegh), the Iranian parliament voted to nationalize the British-owned oil industry, leading to the [Abadan Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abadan_Crisis). Despite British pressure, including an economic blockade, nationalization continued. Mosaddegh was removed from power in 1952 but was quickly re-appointed by the Shah, due to a popular uprising in support of the premier. He forced the Shah into a brief exile in August 1953 after a failed military coup by [Imperial Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Guard_\(Iran\)) Colonel [Nematollah Nassiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nematollah_Nassiri).&#x20;

On 19 August, a successful [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) was headed by retired army general [Fazlollah Zahedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazlollah_Zahedi), aided by the US ([CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA))[^cite_note-BBC-105] and the British ([MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6)), known as Operation Ajax and Operation Boot to the respective agencies.[^cite_note-106] The coup—with a [black propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_propaganda) campaign against Mosaddegh[^cite_note-107]—forced Mosaddegh from office. Mosaddegh was arrested and tried for treason. Found guilty, Mosaddegh had his sentence reduced to house arrest on his family estate while his foreign minister, [Hossein Fatemi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hossein_Fatemi), was executed. Zahedi succeeded him as prime minister and suppressed opposition to the Shah, specifically from the [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(Iran\)) and the communist [Tudeh Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudeh_Party).&#x20;

From that time until the revolution, Iran was ruled as an autocracy under the Shah, with strong American support. Iran initiated economic, social, agrarian, and administrative reforms to modernize the country, which became known as the [White Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Revolution). Many Islamic leaders criticized these initiatives, and the land reform had mixed results.[^cite_note-108] By 1978, the Shah had become wildly unpopular among the Iranian people. Daily demonstrations destabilized the region, and the Shah established [martial law](50477.md) to curb opposition. When hundreds of thousands of protestors persisted, security forces opened fire on the crowds in an incident that became known as [Black Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Friday_\(1978\)).[^cite_note-109]&#x20;

### Islamic Revolution

Main article: [Iranian Revolution](347268.md)



[Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md) returns to Iran after 14 years of [exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah_Khomeini%27s_life_in_exile) in Turkey, Iraq and France on 1 February 1979.

The protests against the Shah grew to include more than 10% of the country; it is rare for revolutions to involve even 1% of a country's population.[^cite_note-110] Frustration with the Shah was so great that even secular and leftist groups supported [Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md), the leader of the [revolution](347268.md), despite sharing none of his political positions.[^cite_note-111] Under increasing pressure, the Shah eventually fled Iran, charging the interim [Regency Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regency_Council_\(Iran\)) with his duties until a new government was formed.[^cite_note-Chamber-112][^cite_note-113] [Shapour Bakhtiar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapour_Bakhtiar), the head of the council, invited Khomeini to return from exile. However, upon return, Khomeini pledged to defeat the interim government led by Bakhtiar and claimed to support free democratic elections. Violence broke out between the two factions, ultimately leading Bakhtiar to flee Iran in disguise.&#x20;

On 31 March 1979, a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Iranian_Islamic_Republic_referendum) was held on whether to transition from a government of monarchy to an [Islamic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_republic). The referendum was approved by a massive margin of 99.31%. It mandated the creation of an [assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_for_the_Final_Review_of_the_Constitution) to draft the new [theocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocratic) constitution, whereby Khomeini became [Supreme Leader](36814344.md) in December 1979. Iran's modernizing, capitalist economy was replaced by populist Islamic economic and cultural policies. Industries were nationalized, laws and schools [Islamized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_knowledge), and [Western influence](21208200.md) restricted.[^cite_note-Britannica_Khomeini-114]&#x20;

#### Khomeini era, Iran–Iraq War, and leadership transition

See also: [Aftermath of the Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_the_Iranian_Revolution) and [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War)



An Iranian soldier with gas mask during the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War)

The shah, now out of power, went to the US to seek cancer treatment after other countries denied him entry. Supporters of the revolution feared that this was a step toward a coup to reinstate the Shah's reign. On 4 November 1979, Iranian students [seized US embassy personnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis), labeling the embassy a "den of spies".[^cite_note-carterpbs-115] 52 hostages were held for 444 days until January 1981.[^cite_note-116] During the [Cultural Revolution in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution_in_Iran) from 1980 to 1983, opposition to Islamic values was purged[^cite_note-z079-117] including through the [1981–1982 Iran massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981%E2%80%931982_Iran_massacres).[^cite_note-b372-118]&#x20;

Iraqi leader [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) attempted to take advantage of the disorder of the revolution, the weakness of the Iranian military and the revolution's antagonism against Western governments. On 22 September 1980, the Iraqi army [invaded Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_invasion_of_Iran) at [Khuzestan](448079.md), precipitating the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War). The attack took revolutionary Iran by surprise. Although Hussein's forces made early advances, Iranian forces pushed the Iraqi army back into Iraq by 1982.[^cite_note-Blair-119]&#x20;

Khomeini sought to [export the revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exporting_the_revolution) westward into Iraq, especially to Iraq's Shi'a Arab majority. The war continued until 1988, when Khomeini "drank the cup of poison" and accepted a truce mediated by the UN.[^cite_note-Blair-119] The war, which ended in a stalemate in 1988, killed approximately 500,000 people.[^cite_note-120] During the war, Saddam extensively used [chemical weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_chemical_weapons_program) against Iranians.[^cite_note-121]&#x20;

#### Khamenei era

Further information: [2009 Iranian presidential election protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Iranian_presidential_election_protests), [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action), [Assassination of Qasem Soleimani](62726668.md), [Mahsa Amini protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahsa_Amini_protests), [2024 Iran–Israel conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Iran%E2%80%93Israel_conflict), [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War), [2025–2026 Iranian protests](81976180.md), [2026 Iran War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Iran_War), [Assassination of Ali Khamenei](82537558.md), and [2026 Iranian supreme leader election](59042456.md)

On his deathbed in 1989, Khomeini appointed a 25-man Constitutional Reform Council, which named Ali Khamenei as the next Supreme Leader, and made changes to Iran's constitution.[^cite_note-122] While Khamenei lacked Khomeini's "charisma and clerical standing", he developed a network of supporters within the armed forces and its economically powerful [religious foundations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonyad).[^cite_note-123]&#x20;



Ali Khamenei at the Great Conference of [Basij](2892082.md) members at [Azadi Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azadi_Stadium), October 2018

President [Akbar Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar_Rafsanjani) concentrated on a pro-business policy of rebuilding the economy, without breaking with the ideology of the revolution. He supported a free market, favoring privatization of state industries and a moderate position internationally. In 1997, Rafsanjani was succeeded by moderate [reformist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_reform_movement) [Mohammad Khatami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Khatami), whose government advocated freedom of expression, constructive diplomatic relations with Asia and the EU, and policy that supported a free market and foreign investment.&#x20;

The [2005 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Iranian_presidential_election) brought conservative populist and nationalist candidate [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](7907317.md) to power. He was known for his hardline views, nuclearization, and [hostility towards Israel](3731122.md), [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), the UK, US and other states. He was the first president to be summoned by the [parliament](383276.md) to answer questions regarding his presidency.[^cite_note-124] In 2013, centrist and reformist [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Rouhani) was elected president. He encouraged personal freedom, access to information, and improved women's rights. He improved diplomatic relations through exchanging conciliatory letters.[^cite_note-125]&#x20;

In 2015, the [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Comprehensive_Plan_of_Action) was reached in [Vienna](55866.md), between Iran, the [P5+1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P5%2B1) ([UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council) + Germany) and the EU. The negotiations centered around ending the [economic sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1929) in exchange for Iran's restriction in producing [enriched uranium](37555.md).[^cite_note-126] In 2018, the US under President [Donald Trump](52231341.md) withdrew from the deal and new sanctions were imposed. This nulled the economic provisions and brought Iran to [nuclear threshold status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_latency).[^cite_note-127]&#x20;

In 2020, [IRGC](1285472.md) general [Qasem Soleimani](16805217.md), the 2nd-most powerful person in Iran,[^cite_note-128] was [assassinated by the US](62726668.md), heightening [tensions between them](310477.md).[^cite_note-Roelants-129] [Iran retaliated against US airbases in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Martyr_Soleimani), the largest ballistic missile attack ever on Americans;[^cite_note-130] 110 sustained [brain injuries](1057414.md).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133] On 8 January, Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps shot down [Ukraine International Airlines Flight 752](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_International_Airlines_Flight_752), killing 176 civilians and leading to [nation-wide protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine_International_Airlines_Flight_752_protests). An international investigation led to the government admitting to the shootdown by a surface-to-air missile.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135]&#x20;

Hardliner [Ebrahim Raisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebrahim_Raisi) lost a presidential bid in 2017, but ran again [in 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Iranian_presidential_election), succeeding Rouhani.[^cite_note-136] During Raisi's term, Iran [intensified uranium enrichment](721807.md), hindered international inspections, joined SCO and BRICS, [supported the](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_and_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) and restored relations with Saudi Arabia. In April 2024, an [Israeli airstrike on the Iranian consulate in Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_airstrike_on_the_Iranian_consulate_in_Damascus) killed an IRGC commander.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138] Iran [retaliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2024_Iranian_strikes_on_Israel) with [UAVs](58900.md), [cruise](6590.md) and [ballistic missiles](38025.md); 9 hit Israel.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] It was the largest drone strike in history,[^cite_note-142] biggest missile attack in Iranian history,[^cite_note-143] its first ever direct attack on Israel[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145] and the first time [since 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Iraqi_missile_attacks_against_Israel) that Israel was directly attacked by a state.[^cite_note-146]&#x20;

In May 2024, Raisi was killed in [a helicopter crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Varzaqan_helicopter_crash),[^cite_note-147] and Iran held a [presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Iranian_presidential_election), when reformist and former [Minister of Health](24234168.md), [Masoud Pezeshkian](2650383.md), was elected.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149] In October, Iran [launched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2024_Iranian_strikes_on_Israel) 180 ballistic missiles at Israel in retaliation for assassinations [of Ismail Haniyeh](77478235.md), [Hassan Nasrallah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Hezbollah_headquarters_strike) and [Abbas Nilforoushan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_Nilforoushan). Israel [struck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2024_Israeli_strikes_on_Iran) Iranian military sites.[^cite_note-150] In May 2025, Iran's government ordered [a mass deportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Afghan_deportation_from_Iran) of an estimated four million [Afghan migrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_diaspora) and refugees living in Iran.[^cite_note-151]&#x20;



Ayatollah [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) with [Saudi Arabia](349303.md)'s Defense Minister [Khalid bin Salman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalid_bin_Salman_Al_Saud) and Major General [Mohammad Bagheri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Bagheri_\(general\)) on 17 April 2025

The weakening of Iran's [key allies and proxies](36658251.md) since 2023 left Iran's government weakened and isolated.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] In early 2025, Iran was rapidly advancing its nuclear program.[^cite_note-155] Analysts warned such activity exceeded any plausible civilian justification.[^cite_note-Economist20250128-156] Iran and the United States [entered negotiations](79685207.md) for a new nuclear agreement, but progress stalled. In June, [IAEA](14984.md) found Iran non-compliant with its nuclear obligations;[^cite_note-Euronews20250612-157] in response, Iran announced activation of a new enrichment facility.[^cite_note-CNN20250612-158]&#x20;

On 13 June 2025, Israel launched strikes across Iran, targeting nuclear facilities and killing top members of Iran's military leadership.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] Iran retaliated with missile strikes, and hostilities resulted in [direct conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War) between them. On 22 June, the [United States struck the Iranian nuclear facilities](80262844.md).[^cite_note-161] Iran then [attacked US bases in Qatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Iranian_strikes_on_Al_Udeid_Air_Base), having given the US and Qatar advance notice hours before the strikes. Qatar said its air defences successfully intercepted the missiles, and no casualties were reported.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163]&#x20;

On 24 June, Israel and Iran agreed to a ceasefire after insistence from the US.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]&#x20;

Since December 2025, Iran [has had mass demonstrations](81976180.md) across cities calling for the overthrow of the Islamic Republic, sparked by frustration over [the economic crisis](78620967.md).[^cite_note-166] As of 13 January 2026, *[Iran International](58555280.md)* estimated that at least 12,000 protesters had been killed amidst the [internet blackout](82063825.md), as government security forces escalated their use of lethal force against demonstrators.[^cite_note-2000killed-167][^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] As of 17 January 2026, estimates suggested that at least 16,500 people had been killed and approximately 330,000 injured in the [2026 Iran massacres](82036668.md).[^cite_note-170]&#x20;

On 28 February 2026, the United States and Israel conducted [coordinated military strikes](82546969.md) against multiple targets in the Islamic Republic of Iran, marking a significant escalation in long-standing tensions between Tehran and the [U.S.–Israel alliance.](3449549.md)[^cite_note-171] That same day, Ali Khamenei was assassinated as part of a series of Israeli airstrikes [aimed at high-ranking Iranian officials](82542668.md). Khamenei's death was confirmed by the Iranian government on 1 March.[^cite_note-172] On 8 March, the 88-person Assembly of Experts announced that Mojtaba Khamenei, son of Ali Khamenei, was elected Supreme Leader in a "unanimous vote".[^cite_note-EasternHerald2026-173][^cite_note-Guardian2026-174][^cite_note-AlJazeera2026-175]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Iran](64697.md)

See also: [Borders of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borders_of_Iran), [Agriculture in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Iran), [List of mountains in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mountains_in_Iran), and [List of islands of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Iran)



The [Topography of Iran](64697.md#Topography)

Iran has an area of 1,648,195 km<sup>2</sup> (636,372 mi<sup>2</sup>), making it is the [sixth-largest country entirely in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_area) and the second-largest in West Asia.[^cite_note-176] It lies between latitudes 24° and 40° N and longitudes 44° and 64° E. It is bordered to the northwest by Armenia, the Azeri exclave of [Nakhchivan](385358.md),[^cite_note-177] and the Republic of Azerbaijan; to the north by the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md); to the northeast by Turkmenistan; to the east by Afghanistan and Pakistan; to the south by the [Persian Gulf](24761.md) and the [Gulf of Oman](12896.md); and to the west by Iraq and Turkey.&#x20;

Iran is in a seismically active area.[^cite_note-178] On average, an earthquake of [magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moment_magnitude_scale) 7.0 or higher occurs once every ten years.[^cite_note-179] Most earthquakes are shallow-focus and can be very devastating, such as the [2003 Bam earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Bam_earthquake). Iran consists mostly of the [Iranian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Plateau), with its southwestern region on the [Arabian plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_plate).&#x20;

Iran is one of the world's most mountainous countries; its landscape is dominated by rugged mountain ranges that separate [basins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin) or [plateaus](538005.md). The populous western Iran is the most mountainous, with ranges such as the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains), [Zagros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagros_Mountains), and [Alborz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alborz). [Mount Damavand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Damavand) is the highest point, at 5,610 m (18,406 ft), which is the highest volcano in Asia. Iran's mountains have impacted its politics and economics for centuries.&#x20;

Northern Iran is covered by the lush lowland [Caspian Hyrcanian forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyrcanian_forests), near the southern shores of the Caspian Sea. The east consists mostly of [desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert) basins, such as the [Kavir Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dasht-e_Kavir), which is the country's largest desert, and the [Lut Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dasht-e_Loot), as well as salt lakes. The Lut Desert features the highest recorded land skin temperature in the world with 70.7 °C recorded in 2005.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] Large plains are found along the coast of the Caspian and at the north end of the Persian Gulf, where Iran borders the mouth of the [Arvand river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shatt_al-Arab). Smaller, discontinuous plains are found along the remaining coast of the Persian Gulf, [Strait of Hormuz](29005.md), and Gulf of Oman.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186]&#x20;

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Iran)



A [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) map of Iran

Iran's climate is diverse, ranging from [arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert_climate) and [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate) to [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropics) along the Caspian coast and northern forests.[^cite_note-HaftlangLang2003-187] On the north edge of Iran, temperatures rarely fall below freezing and the area remains humid. Summer temperatures rarely exceed 29 °C (84.2 °F).[^cite_note-188] Annual precipitation is 680 mm (26.8 in) in the eastern plains and more than 1,700 mm (66.9 in) in the west. The UN Resident Coordinator for Iran has said that "[water scarcity](15380061.md) poses the most severe human security challenge in Iran today."[^cite_note-189]&#x20;

To the west, settlements in the Zagros basin have lower temperatures and severe winters, with freezing average daily temperatures and heavy snowfall. The east and central basins are arid, with less than 200 mm (7.9 in) of rain and have occasional deserts.[^cite_note-Nicholson2011-190] Average summer temperatures may reach 38 °C (100.4 °F). The southern coastal plains of the Persian Gulf and Gulf of Oman have mild winters, and very humid and hot summers. The annual precipitation ranges from 135 to 355 mm (5.3 to 14.0 in).[^cite_note-Nagarajan2010-191]&#x20;

### Biodiversity

See also: [Wildlife of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Iran), [List of national parks and protected areas of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_and_protected_areas_of_Iran), and [Forests of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forests_of_Iran)



A [Persian leopard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_leopard), native to the [Iranian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Plateau)

Nearly 60% of Iran is mountainous, and less than 10% is forested.[^cite_note-192] About 120 million hectares of forests and fields are government-owned for national exploitation.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] Iran's forests can be divided into five vegetation regions: the Hyrcanian region which forms the green belt of the north side of Iran; the [Turan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turan) region, which are mainly scattered in the center of Iran; the [Zagros region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagros_Mountains), which mainly contains oak forests in the west; the Persian Gulf region, which is scattered in the southern coastal belt; the [Arasbarani region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arasbaran), which contains rare and unique species. More than 8,200 [plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant) species are grown. There are over 200 protected areas to preserve biodiversity and wildlife, with over 30 being the [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_park).&#x20;

Iran's living fauna includes 34 bat species, [Indian grey mongoose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_grey_mongoose), [small Indian mongoose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Indian_mongoose), [golden jackal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_jackal), [Indian wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_wolf), [foxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox), [striped hyena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Striped_hyena), [leopard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopard), [Eurasian lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_lynx), [brown bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear) and [Asian black bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_black_bear). [Ungulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ungulate) species include [wild boar](52255.md), [urial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urial), [Armenian mouflon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_mouflon), [red deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_deer), and [goitered gazelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goitered_gazelle).[^cite_note-Fast2005-195][^cite_note-Firouz2005-196] The critically endangered [Asiatic cheetah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_cheetah) survives only in Iran. Iran lost all its [Asiatic lions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_lion) and the extinct [Caspian tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caspian_tiger) by the early 20th century.[^cite_note-197] Domestic ungulates are represented by [sheep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheep), [goat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goat), [cattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle), [horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse), [water buffalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_buffalo), [donkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donkey) and [camel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camel). Iran is home to more than 570 bird species, including the [pheasant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pheasant), [partridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partridge), [stork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stork), [eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eagle), and [falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon).[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200]&#x20;

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Government of Iran](50913538.md) and [Politics of Iran](64702.md)

See also: [Elections in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Iran)

### Supreme Leader

Main article: [Supreme Leader of Iran](36814344.md)



[Mojtaba Khamenei](23324096.md)\
[Supreme Leader](36814344.md)&#x20;



[Masoud Pezeshkian](2650383.md)\
[President](233913.md)&#x20;



[Mohammad Reza Aref](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Reza_Aref)\
[Vice President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_Iran)&#x20;



Supreme Leader Ayatollah [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) with Pakistan's Prime Minister [Shehbaz Sharif](6502561.md) on 26 May 2025

The Supreme Leader,[^cite_note-201] the *Rahbar*, the Leader of the Revolution or Supreme Leadership Authority, is the [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state) and is responsible for supervision of policy. The president has limited power compared to the *Rahbar*. Key ministers are selected with the *Rahbar*'s agreement, who has the ultimate say on foreign policy.[^cite_note-reuters.com-202] The *Rahbar* is directly involved in ministerial appointments for defense, intelligence and foreign affairs, and other top ministries after submission of candidates from the president.&#x20;

The *Rahbar* directly controls regional policy, with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs tasks limited to protocol and ceremonial occasions. Ambassadors to Arab countries, for example, are chosen by the [Quds Force](4028355.md), which reports to the Rahbar.[^cite_note-english.aawsat.com-203] The *Rahbar* can order laws to be amended.[^cite_note-204] The [Setad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_Imam_Khomeini%27s_Order), a [state-owned enterprise](204768.md) under the *Rahbar*, was valued at \$95 billion in 2013, accounts of which are secret even to the [parliament](383276.md).[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-SetadWins-206]&#x20;

The *Rahbar* is the [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the [Armed Forces](14305018.md),[^cite_note-208] controls military intelligence and security operations,[^cite_note-209] and has the sole power to declare war or peace.[^cite_note-210] The *Rahbar* also appoints the heads of the judiciary, state radio and television networks, commanders of the police and military, and members of the [Guardian Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardian_Council).&#x20;

The [Assembly of Experts](512725.md) is responsible for electing the *Rahbar* and has the power to dismiss him on the basis of qualifications and popular esteem.[^cite_note-loc-211] To date, the Assembly of Experts has not challenged any of the *Rahbar*'s decisions nor attempted to dismiss him. The previous head of the judicial system, [Sadiq Larijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadiq_Larijani), appointed by the *Rahbar*, said that it is illegal for the Assembly of Experts to supervise the *Rahbar*.[^cite_note-Al-awsat-212] Many believe the Assembly of Experts has become a ceremonial body without any real power.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215] In February 2025, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported that according to [Karim Sadjadpour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Sadjadpour), an expert on Iran at the [Carnegie Endowment for International Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Endowment_for_International_Peace), there exist in the Islamic Republic of Iran two parallel systems.[^cite_note-Sanger-2025-216] One system is ruled by the military and intelligence forces, who report to the *Rahbar*, and "who oversee the nuclear programme and regional proxies and are tasked with [repression](80259436.md), hostage taking and assassinations".[^cite_note-Sanger-2025-216] The other system is ruled by diplomats and politicians, "who are authorized to speak to Western media and officials" and have minimal knowledge of Iran's nuclear programme.[^cite_note-Sanger-2025-216]&#x20;

The political system is based on the [country's constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Iran).[^cite_note-servcons-217] According to [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance)'s Global State of Democracy (GSoD) Indices and Democracy Tracker, Iran performs in the low range on overall democratic measures, with particular weaknesses in gender equality, freedom of association and assembly, and freedom of expression.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] Furthermore, Iran was ranked 158th in [electoral democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_democracy) for year 2024 according to [V-Dem Democracy Indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices) and was an [electoral autocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_autocracy) in the [Regimes of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices#Regimes_of_the_World) classification.[^cite_note-j496-221][^cite_note-d672-222] [Juan José Linz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Linz) wrote in 2000 that "the Iranian regime combines the ideological bent of [totalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism) with the limited pluralism of [authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism)".[^cite_note-223]&#x20;

### President

Main article: [President of Iran](233913.md)



Iranian President [Masoud Pezeshkian](2650383.md) (second from the right) poses for a group photo with other heads of state during the [16th BRICS summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_BRICS_summit) in [Kazan](202295.md), Russia, on 23 October 2024



The entrance to the [Presidential Administration](46410970.md) palace in [Pasteur Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasteur_Street), which is the cabinet's meeting place, and the [President](233913.md)'s office

The president is [head of government](37570.md) and the second-highest-ranking authority after the Supreme Leader. The president is elected by [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) for 4 years. Before [elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Iran), nominees to become a presidential candidate must be approved by the [Guardian Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardian_Council).[^cite_note-224] The council's members are chosen by the Leader, with the Leader having the power to dismiss the president.[^cite_note-225] The president can only be re-elected for one term.[^cite_note-photius-226] The president is the chairman of the [Supreme National Security Council](6295948.md), and has the power to declare a [state of emergency](298608.md) after passage by the parliament.&#x20;

The president is responsible for the implementation of the constitution and for the exercise of executive powers in implementing the decrees and general policies as outlined by the *Rahbar*, except for matters directly related to the *Rahbar*, who has the final say.[^cite_note-leader-227] The president functions as the executive of affairs such as signing treaties and other international agreements, and administering national planning, budget, and state employment affairs, all as approved by the *Rahbar*.[^cite_note-Middle_East_Eye-228][^cite_note-en.iranwire.com-229] The president appoints ministers, subject to the approval of the Parliament and the *Rahbar*, who can dismiss or reinstate any minister.[^cite_note-stalbertgazette.com-230][^cite_note-news.bbc.co.uk-231][^cite_note-232] The president supervises the [Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_of_Iran), coordinates government decisions, and selects government policies to be placed before the legislature.[^cite_note-233] Eight vice presidents serve under the president, as well as a cabinet of 22 ministers, all appointed by the president.[^cite_note-Judiciary-234]&#x20;

### Guardian Council

Main article: [Guardian Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardian_Council)

Presidential and parliamentary candidates must be approved by the 12-member Guardian Council (all members of which are appointed by the Leader) or the Leader before running to ensure their allegiance.[^cite_note-235] The Leader rarely does the vetting, but has the power to do so, in which case additional approval of the Guardian Council is not needed. The Leader can revert the decisions of the Guardian Council.[^cite_note-236]&#x20;
