### Ethnic groups

Main article: [Ethnicities in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicities_in_Iran)



Colour-coded map of Iran's ethnic groups

Ethnic group composition remains a point of debate, mainly regarding the largest and second-largest ethnic groups, the Persians and Azerbaijanis, due to the lack of Iranian state censuses based on ethnicity.&#x20;

According to a 2003 estimate, [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persians) make up 51% of the population, while [Azerbaijanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Azerbaijanis) make up 24%, [Gilaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilaks) and [Mazanderanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazanderani_people) 8%, [Kurds](3821779.md) 7%, [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Arabs) 3%, [Lurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lurs) 2%, [Balochis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baloch_people) 2%, [Turkmens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Turkmens) 2%, and other groups (including [Armenians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Armenians), [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Jews), [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrians_in_Iran), [Qashqai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qashqai_people), [Shahsevan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahsevan)) make up the remaining 1%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETohidi2009300-571] [The World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook) at 2011 gives the same figures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnited_States2011316-572] The [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress) in 2008 issued slightly different estimates: 65% Persians (also including Gilaks and Mazenderanis[^cite_note-574]), 16% Azerbaijanis, 7% Kurds, 6% Lurs, 2% Arabs, 2% Balochis, 1% Turkic tribal groups (such as Qashqai), and non-Persian, non-Turkic groups (including Armenians, Assyrians, and [Georgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Georgians)) less than 1%.[^cite_note-575]&#x20;

Minorities in Iran have been disproportionately affected by the ongoing [crackdown aimed at repressing](80259436.md) the "Woman, Life, Freedom" movement, according to the UN Fact-Finding Mission.[^cite_note-576]&#x20;

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Iran)



"I am [Cyrus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_the_Great) the king, an [Achaemenid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire)", in [Old Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Persian_cuneiform), [Elamite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elamite_language) and [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language) languages; [Pasargadae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasargadae), [World Heritage Site](44940.md)

Most of the population speaks [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language), Iran's [official](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) and [national language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_language).[^cite_note-AO-577] Others include speakers of other [Iranian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_languages), within the greater [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages) family, and languages belonging to other ethnicities. The [Gilaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilaki_language) and [Mazenderani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazanderani_language) languages are widely spoken in [Gilan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilan_Province) and [Mazenderan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazandaran_Province), northern Iran. The [Talysh language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talysh_language) is spoken in parts of Gilan. Varieties of [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdish_languages) are concentrated in the province of [Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan_Province) and nearby areas. In [Khuzestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestan_Province), [several dialects of Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_dialects_in_Khuzestan) are spoken. South Iran also houses the [Luri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luri_language) and [Lari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achomi_language) languages.&#x20;

[Azerbaijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijani_language), the most-spoken minority language in Iran,[^cite_note-578] and other [Turkic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages) and dialects are found in various regions, especially [Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_\(Iran\)). Notable minority languages include [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language), [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_language), [Neo-Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Aramaic_languages), and [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic). [Khuzi Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestani_Arabic) is spoken by the [Arabs in Khuzestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahwazi_Arabs), and the wider group of [Iranian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Arabs). [Circassian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassian_languages) was once widely spoken by the large Circassian minority, but, due to assimilation, no sizable number of Circassians speak the language anymore.[^cite_note-579][^cite_note-580][^cite_note-581][^cite_note-582]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Iran)



The [*Imam Reza* shrine](14030191.md) in [Mashhad](406512.md) is the most sacred site in Iran and a major center of [pilgrimage for Shia Muslims](21935937.md), containing the mausoleum of the [eighth Imam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_al-Rida).

[Twelver Shi'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver_Shi%27ism) [Islam](6037917.md) is the [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion), which 90–95% of Iranians adhere to according to both official and unofficial sources, such as the CIA.[^cite_note-583][^cite_note-584][^cite_note-585][^cite_note-586][^cite_note-587] About 5–10% of the population follow the [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) branch of Islam, mainly the [Hanafi school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi_school).[^cite_note-588][^cite_note-589]&#x20;

The Sunni branch of Islam, [Christianity](5211.md), [Judaism](15624.md), and [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism) are officially recognized by the government and have reserved seats in the Parliament.[^cite_note-Colin_Brock_p_99-590] There is a large population of adherents to [Yarsanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarsanism), a [Kurdish](3821779.md) indigenous religion, estimated to comprise 500,000 to 1,000,000 followers.[^cite_note-591][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamzeh'ee199039-592][^cite_note-593][^cite_note-594][^cite_note-595] The [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith) is not officially recognized and has been subject to official persecution; the [persecution of Baháʼís](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%ADs) has increased since the revolution.[^cite_note-fdih2-596][^cite_note-ihrdc-597][^cite_note-598] Freedom of religion is limited, [irreligion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) is not recognized by the government and conversion away from Islam is persecuted.[^cite_note-u715-599] Iran is home to the largest Jewish community in the Muslim world and the Middle East, outside of Israel.[^cite_note-600][^cite_note-601] Around 250,000 to 370,000 [Christians reside in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Iran), and it is the largest recognized minority religion. Most are of Armenian background, as well as a sizable minority of Assyrians.[^cite_note-IRFR2009-Iran-602][^cite_note-603][^cite_note-604] The Iranian government has supported the rebuilding and renovation of Armenian churches and has supported the [Armenian Monastic Ensembles of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Monastic_Ensembles_of_Iran). In 2019, the government registered the [Vank Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vank_Cathedral) in [Isfahan](223149.md) as a [World Heritage Site](44940.md). Three Armenian churches in Iran are included in the World Heritage List.[^cite_note-605][^cite_note-606]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Iran](1332627.md)



[University of Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tehran)**,** the oldest Iranian university (1851), and among the [world's top universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_and_university_rankings)

Education is highly centralized. [K–12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%E2%80%9312) is supervised by the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(Iran\)). Higher education is supervised by the [Ministry of Science and Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Science,_Research_and_Technology_\(Iran\)). Literacy among people aged 15 and older was 86% as of 2016, with men (90%) significantly more literate than women (81%). Government expenditure on education is around 4% of GDP.[^cite_note-607]&#x20;

The requirement to enter into higher education is to have a [high school diploma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_school_diploma) and pass the [Iranian University Entrance Exam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_University_Entrance_Exam). Many students do a one–two-year course of [pre-university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University-preparatory_school).[^cite_note-608] Iran's higher education has different levels of diplomas, including an associate degree in two years, a bachelor's degree in four years, and a master's degree in two years, after which another exam allows the candidate to pursue a doctoral programme.[^cite_note-wes.org-609]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Iran)

See also: [Healthcare in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Iran)



[Razavi Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Razavi_Hospital), accredited by ACI

[^cite_note-610]

&#x20;for its quality health services

[^cite_note-611]

Healthcare is provided by the public-governmental system, the private sector, and [NGOs](46539.md).[^cite_note-612] Iran is the only country in the world with a [legal organ trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organ_trade#Iran).[^cite_note-613] Iran has been able to extend public health [preventive services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preventive_medicine) through the establishment of an extensive [Primary Health Care Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_Health_Centre). As a result, child and maternal mortality rates have fallen significantly, and life expectancy at birth has risen. Iran's medical knowledge rank is 17th globally and 1st in the Middle East and North Africa. In terms of medical science production index, Iran ranks 16th in the world.[^cite_note-614] Iran is fast emerging as a preferred destination for [medical tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_tourism).[^cite_note-615]&#x20;

Iran faces the common problem of other young demographic nations in the region, which is keeping pace with growth of an already huge demand for public services. An anticipated increase in the population growth rate will increase the need for public health infrastructures and services.[^cite_note-616] In 2016, about 90% of Iranians had health insurance.[^cite_note-617]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Iran](965233.md)

### Art

Main articles: [Iranian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_art), [Arts of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_of_Iran), and [Iranian modern and contemporary art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_modern_and_contemporary_art)



[Kamal-ol-molk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamal-ol-molk)'s *[Mirror Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_Hall) of [Golestan Palace](313051.md),* often considered a starting point in [Iranian modern art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_modern_and_contemporary_art)

[^cite_note-618]

Iran has one of the richest art heritages in history and been strong in many media including [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture), [painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painting), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music), [metalworking](266443.md), [stonemasonry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonemasonry), [weaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaving), [calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calligraphy) and [sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sculpture). At different times, influences from neighboring civilizations have been important, and latterly Persian art gave and received major influences as part of the wider styles of [Islamic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_art).&#x20;

From the Achaemenid Empire (550–330 BC), the courts of successive dynasties led the style of Persian art, and court-sponsored art left many of the most impressive pieces that remain. The Islamic style of dense decoration, geometrically laid out, developed in Iran into an elegant and harmonious style, combining motifs derived from plants with Chinese motifs such as the cloud-band, and often animals represented at a smaller scale. During the Safavid Empire in the 16th century, this style was used across a variety of media, and diffused from the court artists of the king, most being painters.[^cite_note-619]&#x20;

By the time of the Sasanians, Iranian art had a renaissance.[^cite_note-620] During the Middle Ages, Sasanian art played a prominent role in the formation of European and Asian mediaeval art.[^cite_note-621][^cite_note-622][^cite_note-Iran_in_Britannica-623][^cite_note-624] The Safavid era is known as the Golden Age of Iranian art.[^cite_note-625] [Safavid art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_art) exerted noticeable influences upon the [Ottomans](22278.md), the [Mughals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), and the [Deccans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_sultanates), and was influential through its fashion and garden architecture on 11th–17th-century Europe.&#x20;

Iran's [contemporary art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_modern_and_contemporary_art) traces its origins to [Kamal-ol-molk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamal-ol-molk), a prominent [realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)) painter at the [Qajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) court who affected the norms of painting and adopted a naturalistic style that would compete with photographic works. A new Iranian school of fine art was established by him in 1928, and was followed by the so-called "coffeehouse" style of painting. Iran's [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde) modernists emerged by the arrival of new western influences during World War II. The contemporary art scene originates in the late 1940s, and Tehran's first modern art gallery, Apadana, was opened in 1949 by Mahmud Javadipur, Hosein Kazemi, and Hushang Ajudani.[^cite_note-626] The new movements received official encouragement by the 1950s,[^cite_note-IrMo-627] which led to the emergence of artists such as [Marcos Grigorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_Grigorian).[^cite_note-628]&#x20;

### Architecture

Main articles: [Iranian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_architecture) and [Persian gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_gardens)



[Chehel Sotoun Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chehel_Sotoun) in [Isfahan](223149.md), built during the [Safavid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran) with example of a [talar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talar)*,* [World Heritage Site](44940.md)

The history of architecture in Iran dates back to at least 5,000 BC, with characteristic examples distributed over an area from what is now Turkey and Iraq to Uzbekistan and Tajikistan, and from the Caucasus to Zanzibar. The Iranians made early use of mathematics, geometry and astronomy in their architecture, yielding a tradition with structural and aesthetic variety.[^cite_note-629] The guiding [motif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motif_\(visual_arts\)) is its cosmic symbolism.[^cite_note-630]&#x20;

In addition to historic gates, palaces, and mosques, the rapid growth of cities such as Tehran has brought a wave of construction. Iran ranks 7th among UNESCO's list of countries with the most archaeological ruins and attractions from antiquity.[^cite_note-631]&#x20;

### World Heritage Sites

Main articles: [List of World Heritage Sites in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Iran) and [Iran National Heritage List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_National_Heritage_List)

Iran's rich culture and history is reflected by its 29 [World Heritage Sites](44940.md), ranking 1st in the Middle East, and 10th in the world. These include [Persepolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persepolis), [Naghsh-e Jahan Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqsh-e_Jahan_Square), [Chogha Zanbil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chogha_Zanbil), [Pasargadae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasargadae), [Golestan Palace](313051.md), [Arg-e Bam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arg-e_Bam), [Behistun Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behistun_Inscription), [Shahr-e Sukhteh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahr-e_Sukhteh), [Susa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susa), [Takht-e Soleyman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takht-e_Soleym%C4%81n), [Hyrcanian forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyrcanian_forests), the city of [Yazd](451385.md) and more. Iran has 26 [Intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_cultural_heritage), or Human treasures, which ranks 4th worldwide.[^cite_note-632][^cite_note-633]&#x20;

### Weaving

Main articles: [Persian carpet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_carpet), [Tabriz rug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabriz_rug), and [Ardabil Carpet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardabil_Carpet)



The [Pazyryk Carpet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pazyryk_burials#Pazyryk_rug), circa 400 BC, is considered world's the oldest known surviving carpet

Iran's carpet-weaving has its origins in the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) and is one of the most distinguished manifestations of Iranian art. Carpet weaving is an essential part of [Persian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_culture) and [Iranian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_art). Persian rugs and carpets were woven in parallel by nomadic tribes in village and town workshops, and by royal court manufactories. As such, they represent simultaneous lines of tradition, and reflect the history of Iran, Persian culture, and its various peoples. Although the term "Persian carpet" most often refers to pile-woven textiles, flat-woven carpets and rugs like [Kilim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilim), [Soumak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soumak_rug), and embroidered tissues like [Suzani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzani_\(textile\)) are part of the manifold tradition of Persian carpet weaving.&#x20;

Iran produces three-quarters of the world's handmade carpets, and has 30% of export markets.[^cite_note-Goswami2009-634][^cite_note-635] In 2010, the "traditional skills of carpet weaving" in [Fars province](448049.md) and [Kashan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashan) were inscribed to the [UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Intangible_Cultural_Heritage_Lists) List.[^cite_note-636][^cite_note-637][^cite_note-638] Within the [Oriental rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_rug) produced by the countries of the "rug belt", the Persian carpet stands out by the variety and elaborateness of its manifold designs.[^cite_note-639]&#x20;

Carpets woven in towns and regional centers like [Tabriz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabriz_rug), [Kerman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerman_carpet), [Ravar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravar), [Neyshabour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neyshabour), [Mashhad](406512.md), [Kashan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashan), [Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan_rug), [Nain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nain_rug) and [Qom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qom_rug) are characterized by their specific weaving techniques and use of high-quality materials, colours and patterns. Hand-woven Persian rugs and carpets have been regarded as objects of high artistic value and prestige, since they were mentioned by [ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) writers.&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [Literature in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_in_Iran)

See also: [Iranian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_literature)



Tombs of [Hafez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez)

Iran's oldest literary tradition is that of [Avestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avestan), the [Old Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_languages#Old_Iranian) [sacred language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_language) of the [Avesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avesta), which consists of the legendary and religious texts of Zoroastrianism and the [ancient Iranian religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Iranian_religion).[^cite_note-640][^cite_note-641] The Persian language was used and developed through [Persianate societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persianate_society) in Asia Minor, Central Asia, and South Asia, leaving extensive influences on [Ottoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_poetry) and [Mughal literatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language_in_the_Indian_subcontinent), among others. Iran has several famous medieval poets, notably [Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumi), [Ferdowsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdowsi), [Hafez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez), [Saadi Shirazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saadi_Shirazi), [Omar Khayyam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Khayyam), and [Nizami Ganjavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizami_Ganjavi).[^cite_note-642]&#x20;

Described as one of the great literatures of humanity,[^cite_note-643] including [Goethe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goethe)'s assessment of it as one of the four main bodies of world literature,[^cite_note-644] Persian literature has its roots in surviving works of [Middle Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Persian) and [Old Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Persian), the latter of which dates back as far as 522 BC, the date of the earliest surviving Achaemenid inscription, the [Behistun Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behistun_Inscription). The bulk of surviving Persian literature, however, comes from the times following the Muslim conquest in c. 650 AD. After the Abbasids came to power (750 AD), the Iranians became the scribes and bureaucrats of the Islamic Caliphate and, increasingly, also its writers and poets. The New Persian language literature arose and flourished in Khorasan and Transoxiana because of political reasons, early Iranian dynasties of post-Islamic Iran, such as the Tahirids and Samanids, being based in Khorasan.[^cite_note-645]&#x20;

### Museums

Main article: [List of museums in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Iran)



The [National Museum of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Iran) in Tehran

The [National Museum of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Iran) in Tehran is the country's most important cultural institution. As the first and biggest museum in Iran, the institution includes the [Museum of Ancient Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Ancient_Iran) and the [Museum of the Islamic Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_the_Islamic_Era). The National Museum is the world's most important museum in terms of preservation, display and research of archaeological collections of Iran,[^cite_note-National_Museum_of_Iran-646] and ranks as one of the few most prestigious museums globally in terms of volume, diversity and quality of its monuments.[^cite_note-National_Museum_of_Iran-646]&#x20;

There are many other popular museums across the country such as the [Golestan Palace](313051.md) ([World Heritage Site](44940.md)), [The Treasury of National Jewels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasury_of_National_Jewels), [Reza Abbasi Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Abbasi_Museum), [Tehran Museum of Contemporary Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Museum_of_Contemporary_Art), [Sa'dabad Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%27dabad_Complex), [The Carpet Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpet_Museum_of_Iran), [Abgineh Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abgineh_Museum_of_Tehran), [Pars Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pars_Museum), [Azerbaijan Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_Museum), Hegmataneh Museum, Susa Museum and more. Around 25 million people visited the museums in 2019.[^cite_note-647][^cite_note-648]&#x20;

### Music and dance

Main articles: [Music of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Iran) and [Iranian dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_dance)



A [Karnay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karnay), an ancient Iranian musical instrument from the 6th century BC, kept at the Persepolis Museum



Dancers on a piece of ceramic from [Cheshmeh-Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheshmeh-Ali_\(Shahr-e-Rey\)), 5000 BC

Iran is the apparent birthplace of the earliest complex instruments, dating to the 3rd millennium BC.[^cite_note-649] The use of [angular harps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_harp) have been documented at Madaktu and [Kul-e Farah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kul-e_Farah), with the largest collection of Elamite instruments documented at Kul-e Farah. [Xenophon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophon)'s *[Cyropaedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyropaedia)* mentions singing women at the court of the Achaemenid Empire. Under the Parthian Empire, the *[gōsān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gusans)* ([Parthian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_language) for 'minstrel') had a prominent role.[^cite_note-650][^cite_note-651]&#x20;

The history of [Sasanian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_music) is better documented than earlier periods and is especially more evident in Avestan texts.[^cite_note-EI-mhphi-652] By the time of [Khosrow II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khosrow_II), the Sasanian royal court hosted prominent musicians, namely Azad, [Bamshad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamshad), [Barbad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbad), [Nagisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagisa_\(harpist\)), Ramtin, and [Sarkash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarkash). Iranian traditional musical instruments include string instruments such as [chang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang_\(instrument\)) ([harp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harp)), [qanun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanun_\(instrument\)), [santur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santur), [rud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rud) ([oud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oud), [barbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbat_\(lute\))), [tar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tar_\(string_instrument\)), [dotar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutar), [setar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Setar), [tanbur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanbur), and [kamanche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamancheh), wind instruments such as [sorna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorna) ([zurna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurna), [karna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karnay)) and [ney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ney), and percussion instruments such as [tompak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonbak), [kus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kus), [daf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daf) ([dayere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayereh)), and [naqare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqareh).&#x20;

The [Tehran Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Symphony_Orchestra) was founded in 1933. By the late 1940s, [Ruhollah Khaleqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah_Khaleqi) founded the country's first national music society and established the School of National Music in 1949.[^cite_note-653] [Iranian pop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_pop_music) has its origins in the [Qajar era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_Iran).[^cite_note-654] It was significantly developed since the 1950s, using indigenous instruments and forms accompanied by electric guitar and other imported characteristics. [Iranian rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_rock) emerged in the 1960s and [hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_hip_hop) in the 2000s.[^cite_note-655][^cite_note-656]&#x20;

Iran has known dance in the forms of music, play, drama or religious rituals since at least the 6th millennium BC. Artifacts with pictures of dancers were found in archaeological prehistoric sites.[^cite_note-657] Genres of dance vary depending on the area, culture, and language of the local people, and can range from sophisticated reconstructions of refined court dances to energetic folk dances.[^cite_note-658] Each group, region, and historical epoch has specific dance styles associated with it. The earliest researched dance from historic Iran is a dance worshipping Mithra. Ancient Persian dance was significantly researched by Greek historian [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus). Iran was occupied by foreign powers, causing a slow disappearance of heritage dance traditions.&#x20;

The Qajar era had an important influence on Persian dance. In this period, a style of dance began to be called "[classical Persian dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Persian_dance)". Dancers performed artistic dances in court for entertainment purposes such as coronations, marriage celebrations, and [Norouz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz) celebrations. In the 20th century, the music came to be orchestrated and dance movement and costuming gained a modernistic orientation to the West.&#x20;

### Media

Main article: [Media of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Iran)



[IRIB](819903.md), the Iranian [state-controlled](20803507.md) media corporation

Iran's largest media corporation is the state-owned [IRIB](819903.md). The [Ministry of Culture and Islamic Guidance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Culture_and_Islamic_Guidance) is responsible for the cultural policy, including activities regarding communications and information.[^cite_note-659] Most of the [newspapers published in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Iran) are in Persian, the country's official and national language. The country's most widely circulated periodicals are based in Tehran, among which are *[Etemad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etemad)*, *[Ettela'at](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ettela%27at)*, *[Kayhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kayhan)*, *[Hamshahri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamshahri)*, *[Resalat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resalat_\(newspaper\))*, and *[Shargh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shargh)*.[^cite_note-BYI-473] *[Tehran Times](5997270.md)*, *[Iran Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_\(newspaper\))*, and *[Financial Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Tribune)* are among the famous English-language newspapers based in Iran.&#x20;

Iran ranks 17th among [countries by number of Internet users](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_Internet_users). [Google Search](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Search) is Iran's most widely used search engine and [Instagram](31591547.md) is the most popular online [social networking service](2041117.md).[^cite_note-Alexa_Internet-660] Direct access to many worldwide mainstream websites has been blocked in Iran, including [Facebook](7529378.md), which has been blocked since 2009. About 90% of Iran's [e-commerce](9611.md) takes place on the Iranian online store [Digikala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digikala), which has around 750,000 visitors per day and is the most visited online store in the Middle East.[^cite_note-661]&#x20;

Arrests of journalists critical of the government have increased since 2022 according to [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md).[^cite_note-l402-662]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Iranian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_cuisine)



[Chelow kabab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelow_kabab) (rice and [kebab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebab)), one of [Iran's national dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%27s_national_dishes)

Iranian main dishes include varieties of [kebab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebab), [pilaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilaf), [stew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stew) ([khoresh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoresh)), [soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soup) and [āsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80sh), and [omelette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omelette). Lunch and dinner meals are commonly accompanied by side dishes such as plain [yogurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogurt) or [mast-o-khiar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzatziki), [sabzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabzi_khordan), [salad Shirazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirazi_salad), and [torshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tursu), and might follow dishes such as [borani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borani), [Mirza Qasemi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirza_Ghassemi), or [kashk e bademjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashk_e_bademjan). In Iranian culture, tea is widely consumed.[^cite_note-663][^cite_note-664] Iran is the world's seventh major tea producer.[^cite_note-FAOSTAT2-665] One of Iran's most popular desserts is the [falude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faloodeh).[^cite_note-666] There is also the popular saffron ice cream, known as *[Bastani Sonnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bastani_Sonnati)* ("traditional ice cream"),[^cite_note-667] which is sometimes accompanied with [carrot juice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrot_juice).[^cite_note-668] Iran is also famous for its [caviar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caviar).[^cite_note-669]&#x20;

Typical Iranian main dishes are combinations of [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice) with [meat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meat), [vegetables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetable) and [nuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nut_\(fruit\)). [Herbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_culinary_herbs_and_spices) are frequently used, along with fruits such as [plums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plum), [pomegranates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate), [quince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quince), [prunes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prune), [apricots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apricot) and [raisins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raisin). Characteristic Iranian spices and flavourings such as [saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron), [cardamom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardamom), and [dried lime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dried_lime) and other sources of sour flavoring, [cinnamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinnamon), [turmeric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turmeric) and [parsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsley) are mixed and used in various dishes.&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Iran)



[Dizin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dizin), biggest [ski resort](179821.md) in the [Middle East](19323.md)



[Azadi Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azadi_Stadium) in [Tehran](57654.md), [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia)'s largest football stadium

Iran is the birthplace of [polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polo),[^cite_note-670][^cite_note-671][^cite_note-672] locally known as chogan, with its earliest records attributed to the ancient Medes.[^cite_note-673] Freestyle wrestling is traditionally considered the national sport, and [Iran's wrestlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_national_freestyle_wrestling_athletes) have been world champions many times. Iran's traditional wrestling, called *[koڑti e pahlevāni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlevani_and_zoorkhaneh_rituals)* ("heroic wrestling"), is registered on UNESCO's Intangible Cultural Heritage list.[^cite_note-674] Iran's [National Olympic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Olympic_Committee_of_the_Islamic_Republic_of_Iran) was founded in 1947. Wrestlers and weightlifters have achieved the country's highest [records at the Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_at_the_Olympics). In 1974, Iran became the first country in West Asia to host the [Asian Games](39205.md).[^cite_note-675][^cite_note-676][^cite_note-677]&#x20;

As a mountainous country, Iran is a venue for skiing, snowboarding, hiking, rock climbing,[^cite_note-678] and mountain climbing.[^cite_note-679][^cite_note-680] It is home to ski resorts, the most famous being [Tochal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tochal_Complex), [Dizin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dizin), and [Shemshak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shemshak_\(ski_resort\)).[^cite_note-Snowseasoncentral.com_November_29_2015c-681] Dizin is the largest, and authorized by [FIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Ski_and_Snowboard_Federation) to administer international competitions.[^cite_note-682]&#x20;

[Football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Iran) is the most popular sport, with the [men's national team](272865.md) having won the [Asian Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFC_Asian_Cup) three times. The men's team ranks 2nd in Asia and 18th in the [FIFA World Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_World_Rankings) as of April 2025.[^cite_note-683] The [Azadi Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azadi_Stadium) in Tehran is the largest association football stadium in West Asia and on a list of top-20 stadiums in the world.[^cite_note-684] Volleyball is the second most popular sport.[^cite_note-685][^cite_note-686] Having won the 2011 and 2013 [Asian Men's Volleyball Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Men%27s_Volleyball_Championship), the [men's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_men%27s_national_volleyball_team) is the 2nd strongest in Asia, and 15th in the [FIVB World Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIVB_World_Rankings) as of January 2025. [Basketball](3921.md) is also popular, with the [men's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_national_basketball_team) having won three [Asian Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_Asia_Championship) since 2007.[^cite_note-687]&#x20;

[^cite_note-574]: &#x20;"The *Factbook* puts 'Persian and Persian dialects' at 58 percent, but 51 percent of the population as ethnic Persians, while the Library of Congress states that Persian 'is spoken as a mother tongue by at least 65 percent of the population and as a second language by a large proportion of the remaining 35 percent. The 'Persian' mentioned in the latter report must thus also include Gilaki and Mazi. However, Gilaki and Mazi are actually from a different branch of the Iranian language subfamily than Persian, and could be as such be seen not as dialects, but as distinct languages. Suffice it here to say that while some scholars see categories such as Gilakis and Mazandaranis as referring to separate ethnic groups due to their linguistic traits, others count them as 'Persians' on exactly the same basis."<sup>[\[561\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElling201319-573)</sup>
